   14: - **AT-004.04 (P0)** — Given a funded project (fuel balance > 0), When the NGO runs Discovery on it, Then every turn bills the project's fuel — never the free pool ("Funded → all-$"), and the same platform share applies as any consumption [cross: REQ-006].
   15: - **AT-004.05 (P0)** — Given one NGO with a funded project A and an unfunded project B, When it runs Discovery on both, Then A's turns bill A's fuel and B's turns draw the NGO's free daily pool — the pool serves only unfunded projects.

   26: - **AT-004.11 (P0)** — Given a Discovery conversation in progress, When the NGO leaves and returns (new session, next day), Then the conversation persists and resumes with full prior context.

   52: ## E. Data-sensitivity tiers
   53: 
   54: - **AT-004.26 (P0)** — Given any Discovery, When the conversation proceeds, Then the agent asks what data the tool will handle BEFORE assigning a sensitivity tier.
   55: - **AT-004.27 (P0)** — Given ordinary-PII needs, When tiered, Then Tier 1 is assigned with a minimization reminder and the NGO data-responsibility acknowledgment.
   56: - **AT-004.28 (P0)** — Given special-category or high-volume PII, When tiered, Then Tier 2 is assigned: synthetic/anonymized fixtures only during build; the NGO connects real data itself after completion; real Tier-2 data never reaches Anthropic, Lovable, or the volunteer.
   57: - **AT-004.29 (P0)** — Given health, immigration, abuse-victim, or financial data, When tiered, Then the tier is never below Tier 2.
   58: - **AT-004.30 (P0)** — Given genuine uncertainty about the data, When tiered, Then Tier 2 is assigned (unsure → Tier 2).
   59: - **AT-004.31 (P0)** — Given a no-restriction tool (no personal data), When tiered, Then Tier 0 is assigned. [cx: promoted P1→P0]
   60: - **AT-004.50 (P0)** — Given a tier assignment, When it lands, Then the data-exposure responsibility is attributed to the NGO, and a data-responsibility acknowledgment is required in the tier flows that mandate one (Tier 1 and Tier 2) — not universally for Tier 0. [cx r2: acknowledgment only where the tier rules require it]
   61: - **AT-004.51 (P0)** — Given a Discovery-assigned data tier — including one where post-Discovery scope EDITS changed the data surface (second fixture), When the project reaches triage [cross: REQ-023], Then the tier and the final edited scope are presented to the HUMAN reviewer with the advisory pass's data-tier evidence, and the tier is confirmed by the reviewer's decision — never treated as final without human confirmation. [cx: added] [d74: confirmation is the reviewer's, informed by advisory evidence; post-edit drift covered]

   63: ## F. Maintainability-fit check & declines
   64: 
   65: - **AT-004.32 (P0)** — Given a need a non-technical staffer can maintain via Lovable chat (internal tools: intake forms, CRUD trackers, directories, dashboards), When checked, Then it passes the fit check (fit by default).
   66: - **AT-004.33 (P0)** — Given a need requiring ongoing developer maintenance (developer-grade, one-off, pure-backend, or Tier-2 data that cannot live in Lovable), When checked, Then Discovery declines plainly, explains the limitation, and produces NO publishable scope.
   67: - **AT-004.34 (P0)** — Given a confidential-codebase need, When checked, Then it is likewise declined (public-only v1).
   68: - **AT-004.35 (P0)** — Given any decline, When it lands, Then a decline record exists for founder review — and there is no waitlist and no second track.
   69: - **AT-004.36 (P0)** — Given a tool handling sensitive *data* that otherwise fits, When checked, Then it is not declined for sensitivity — it is served, with the tier assigned by the 0/1/2 rules (ordinary PII → Tier 1, special-category → Tier 2). [cx: corrected — not everything sensitive is Tier-2]
   70: - **AT-004.53 (P0)** — Given a fit decline of either cause (ongoing developer maintenance; confidential codebase), When it lands, Then ALL of the following exist from that one event: the NGO's decline message; a durable decline record ON THE PROJECT carrying cause, date and reshaping suggestion; exactly ONE platform-admin ops item carrying the project, the cause and the full Discovery conversation; and the admin notification (REQ-016). A decline that produces the message but no ops item, or two ops items, fails. [d89]
   71: - **AT-004.54 (P0)** — Given a declined project, When the NGO views its project list, Then the declined project is still listed with its decline record visible — the decline is not chat-only. And Given the decline message itself, Then its copy states that a person reviews every decline and will reach out if it was wrong (the oversight sentence is present, not implied). [d89]
   72: - **AT-004.55 (P0)** — Given an open fit-decline ops item, When the founder disposes it **upheld**, Then the item closes, the disposition is recorded with actor and timestamp, the project stays `cancelled`, and the NGO receives NO further notification. [d89]
   73: - **AT-004.56 (P0)** — Given an open fit-decline ops item, When the founder disposes it **overturned**, Then the project returns to `discovery_in_progress` with its Discovery conversation intact and resumable, the NGO is notified that Discovery reopened, and the disposition is recorded. [d89] [cross: REQ-005.5 owns the transition legality]
   74: - **AT-004.57 (P0)** — Given a set of disposed fit-declines, When the calibration dataset is read, Then every disposition (upheld and overturned) is retrievable with its decline cause and Discovery conversation — the dataset the decline evaluator is tuned against, parallel to REQ-023's founder-review records. An unauditable decline — one with no ops item or no recorded disposition path — fails. [d89]

   77: 
   78: - **AT-004.37 (P0)** — Given a generated scope the NGO rejects, When they regenerate, Then regeneration works a bounded number of times, each with a logged reason, and costs zero credits.
   79: - **AT-004.38 (P0)** — Given the regeneration bound is exhausted, When the NGO tries again, Then the case escalates to an admin instead of regenerating.
   80: - **AT-004.39 (P0)** — Given a system-error mid-turn, When the turn is retried, Then the retry costs zero credits.

    1: import { join } from 'node:path';
    2: import { pathToFileURL } from 'node:url';

    4: import { pendingCapability, realCapability, standInCapability, stubbedCapabilityNames, type Capability } from './capabilities.ts';
    5: import { REPO_ROOT } from './check.ts';
    6: import { ControlledClock } from './clock.ts';
    7: import { createConfigRegistry, type ConfigRegistry } from './config.ts';
    8: import type { AtHarness, StaticScan } from './contracts.ts';
    9: import { createFaults, type AdapterFaultSeam } from './faults.ts';
   10: import { createFixtureSeed, FixtureWorldStore } from './fixtures.ts';
   11: import { createSentinels, type AdapterSentinelSeam } from './sentinels.ts';

   15: interface FixtureAdapter {
   16:   fixtures: { world(name: string): Promise<{ teardown(): Promise<void> }> };
   17:   sut: Record<string, unknown>;
   18:   /**
   19:    * OPTIONAL, and refused at use rather than ignored. The runner's own black-box trees write
   20:    * disposable adapters that export `sut`, `fixtures` and `teardown` and nothing else, so a
   21:    * required member here would break them at run time. Absence is not permission to no-op: an
   22:    * adapter that offers no fault seam exposes no fault points, so `faults.at()` refuses through
   23:    * `faultPointProblem` in the guard's own words, and a scan of any scope is refused the same way.
   24:    */
   25:   faults?: AdapterFaultSeam;
   26:   sentinels?: AdapterSentinelSeam;
   27:   teardown(): Promise<void>;
   28: }
   29: 
   30: interface FixtureAdapterModule {
   31:   /** the requirement this adapter declares itself to be, e.g. 'req-016' — see loadAdapter() */
   32:   requirement: string;
   33:   createFixtureAdapter(opts: {
   34:     clock: ControlledClock;
   35:     worlds: FixtureWorldStore;
   36:     config: ConfigRegistry;
   37:     /**
   38:      * The SUT-facing half of H5's provider seam. ADDITIVE on the options object rather than a new
   39:      * required export, so the runner's disposable black-box adapters — which take an options object
   40:      * they largely ignore — keep working untouched.
   41:      */
   42:     vendors: { email: EmailProviderPort };
   43:   }): Promise<FixtureAdapter> | FixtureAdapter;
   44: }
   45: 
   46: /**
   47:  * The adapter is resolved through REPO_ROOT rather than relative to this file, so that the suites
   48:  * directory the harness loads from is the SAME tree the runner and the bijection checker read.
   49:  * They agree by construction, including when `AT_REPO_ROOT` points all three at a disposable
   50:  * fixture tree for the runner's own black-box tests.
   51:  */
   52: function adapterUrl(requirement: string): string {
   53:   return pathToFileURL(join(REPO_ROOT, 'tests', 'at', 'suites', requirement, '_fixture.ts')).href;
   54: }
   55: 
   56: async function loadAdapter(
   57:   requirement: string,
   58:   clock: ControlledClock,
   59:   worlds: FixtureWorldStore,
   60:   config: ConfigRegistry,
   61:   vendors: { email: EmailProviderPort },
   62: ): Promise<FixtureAdapter> {
   63:   const moduleUrl = adapterUrl(requirement);
   64:   let module: Partial<FixtureAdapterModule>;
   65:   try {
   66:     module = (await import(/* @vite-ignore */ moduleUrl)) as Partial<FixtureAdapterModule>;
   67:   } catch (err) {
   68:     const detail = err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err);
   69:     throw new Error(`no fixture adapter for ${requirement} at ${moduleUrl} — ${detail}`);
   70:   }
   71:   if (typeof module.createFixtureAdapter !== 'function') {
   72:     throw new Error(`fixture adapter for ${requirement} exports no createFixtureAdapter()`);
   73:   }
   74: 
   75:   /*
   76:    * THE ADAPTER SAYS WHO IT IS, AND IS HELD TO IT.
   77:    *
   78:    * `harness/suite-adapters.ts` reads a suite's system-under-test and world types from the module
   79:    * its map names, while THIS function loads a module from a path built out of a string. Those were
   80:    * two independent facts: a mistyped map entry, a renamed directory or an `AT_REPO_ROOT` pointing
   81:    * somewhere else would have made the type-check describe one suite while the run drove another,
   82:    * and every layer would have looked correct on its own.
   83:    *
   84:    * The map entry is constrained to match this literal at compile time; the same literal is checked
   85:    * here against the requirement actually requested. So the key, the module the types came from and
   86:    * the module really imported are one self-declared value, checked at both ends.
   87:    *
   88:    * A MISSING literal is an error too, not a skipped check — a guard that switches itself off when a
   89:    * field is absent is the hole again, arriving through the door marked convenience.
   90:    */
   91:   if (module.requirement !== requirement) {
   92:     throw new Error(
   93:       `fixture adapter at ${moduleUrl} declares requirement ` +
   94:         `${module.requirement === undefined ? '<nothing: it exports no `requirement`>' : JSON.stringify(module.requirement)} ` +
   95:         `but was loaded as ${JSON.stringify(requirement)} — the suite's types would be read off one ` +
   96:         `module while the run drove another. Add \`export const requirement = ${JSON.stringify(requirement)} as const;\` ` +
   97:         `to that file, or correct whichever of the two names is wrong.`,
   98:     );
   99:   }
  100: 
  101:   return module.createFixtureAdapter({ clock, worlds, config, vendors });
  102: }
  103: 
  104: /**
  105:  * The return type is annotated, not inferred, and that annotation is load-bearing: it is what makes
  106:  * the shared contract a checked promise rather than a hopeful one. Inferred, this factory produced
  107:  * `object` for every pending seam — `pendingCapability<T extends object>()` has no inference site,
  108:  * so `T` fell back to its constraint — and the result was not assignable to `AtHarness` at all.
  109:  * With the annotation, dropping or misnaming a contract member is a compile error here, where it is
  110:  * written, instead of a surprise in whichever suite reaches for it.
  111:  */
  112: export async function createHarness(opts: {
  113:   requirement: string;
  114:   tier: Tier;
  115:   configOverrides?: ConfigOverrides;
  116: }): Promise<AtHarness> {
  117:   const clock = standInCapability('clock.controlled', new ControlledClock());
  118:   const worlds = new FixtureWorldStore(createFixtureSeed());
  119:   // REAL, not a stand-in: `atconfig.ts` IS the registry of pinned values, so what a test reads
  120:   // here is the article itself. Marking it stand-in would tell an integration-tier run that the
  121:   // gate is grading a substitute, which would be a lie in the other direction.
  122:   const config = realCapability('config.registry', createConfigRegistry(opts.configOverrides));
  123:   // BUILT BEFORE THE ADAPTER, because the adapter is handed the sending half of it. One sim per
  124:   // harness, so the forced-outcome queue and the accepted-identity set cannot survive into the next
  125:   // test — `registry.ts` opens a fresh harness per `open()` and tears it down per id.
  126:   const provider = createEmailProviderSim();
  127:   const adapter = await loadAdapter(opts.requirement, clock.value, worlds, config.value, { email: provider.port });
  128:   const fixtures = standInCapability('fixtures.worlds', adapter.fixtures);
  129:   // REAL, not stand-ins, for the same reason `config.registry` is. `stubbedCapabilities()` reports
  130:   // what the HARNESS substituted, and nothing about H3 is substituted here — the marker store and
  131:   // the fault router below are the article. What is a substitute in a loop-tier run is the product,
  132:   // and `sut.notifications` already declares that; declaring H3 a stand-in as well would count the
  133:   // same substitution twice and blame the wrong layer for it. It would also cost something real:
  134:   // `registry.ts` refuses ANY stubbed capability above the loop tier, so the label would bar this
  135:   // identical machinery from ever running at integration tier — the tier that is the closing gate.
  136:   const sentinels = realCapability('sentinels.planted', createSentinels(adapter.sentinels));
  137:   const faults = realCapability('faults.injection', createFaults(adapter.faults));
  138:   // A STAND-IN BY NATURE, and saying so has teeth: `registry.ts` refuses any stubbed capability
  139:   // above the loop tier, so an integration-tier run cannot silently grade against the simulator.
  140:   // What stands here at that tier is a later slice's decision, not this one's.
  141:   const vendors = standInCapability('vendors.email', { email: provider.sim });
  142:   const sutCapabilities: Capability<unknown>[] = Object.entries(adapter.sut).map(([name, value]) =>
  143:     standInCapability(`sut.${name}`, value),
  144:   );
  145:   const constructed: Capability<unknown>[] = [clock, fixtures, config, sentinels, faults, vendors, ...sutCapabilities];
  146: 
  147:   let tornDown = false;
  148:   return {
  149:     tier: opts.tier,
  150:     stubbedCapabilities: async () => stubbedCapabilityNames(constructed),
  151:     clock: clock.value,
  152:     fixtures: fixtures.value,
  153:     sut: adapter.sut,
  154:     sentinels: sentinels.value,
  155:     faults: faults.value,
  156:     // 'H3 sentinels' went from this list in the change that made planting work, and
  157:     // 'H5 email provider simulator' goes in the change that builds the simulator above. The seam
  158:     // names the whole missing set so its first throw reports all of it at once; the moment one of
  159:     // them lands, keeping its name here is a declared fact that has drifted from a real one.
  160:     // `AT-016.01` stays red — `providerClientImporters()` is what throws — but the reason it is red
  161:     // changed, and `tests/at/expected/req-016.json` states the same one name for the same reason.
  162:     static: pendingCapability<StaticScan>('H3 static provider scan'),
  163:     vendors: vendors.value,
  164:     config: config.value,
  165:     teardown: async () => {
  166:       if (tornDown) return;
  167:       tornDown = true;
  168:       await adapter.teardown();
  169:       await worlds.teardown();
  170:     },
  171:   };
  172: }

 succeeded in 609ms:
    1: export type CapabilityProvenance = 'real' | 'stand-in';
    2: 
    3: const CAPABILITY = Symbol('at-capability');
    4: 
    5: export interface Capability<T> {
    6:   readonly [CAPABILITY]: true;
    7:   readonly name: string;
    8:   readonly provenance: CapabilityProvenance;
    9:   readonly value: T;
   10: }
   11: 
   12: function capability<T>(name: string, provenance: CapabilityProvenance, value: T): Capability<T> {
   13:   if (!name.trim()) throw new Error('a capability factory requires a non-empty name');
   14:   return Object.freeze({ [CAPABILITY]: true as const, name, provenance, value });
   15: }
   16: 
   17: export function realCapability<T>(name: string, value: T): Capability<T> {
   18:   return capability(name, 'real', value);
   19: }
   20: 
   21: export function standInCapability<T>(name: string, value: T): Capability<T> {
   22:   return capability(name, 'stand-in', value);
   23: }
   24: 
   25: export function stubbedCapabilityNames(capabilities: readonly Capability<unknown>[]): string[] {
   26:   return capabilities
   27:     .filter((entry) => entry[CAPABILITY] === true && entry.provenance === 'stand-in')
   28:     .map((entry) => entry.name)
   29:     .sort();
   30: }
   31: 
   32: export class CapabilityPending extends Error {
   33:   constructor(readonly capabilities: readonly string[]) {
   34:     super(`CAPABILITY PENDING — ${capabilities.join(', ')}`);
   35:     this.name = 'CapabilityPending';
   36:   }
   37: }
   38: 
   39: /** A typed seam for later slices. Any attempted use fails with the capability names, never a no-op. */
   40: export function pendingCapability<T extends object>(...capabilities: string[]): T {
   41:   const names = [...new Set(capabilities)];
   42:   return new Proxy(
   43:     {},
   44:     {
   45:       get() {
   46:         throw new CapabilityPending(names);
   47:       },
   48:     },
   49:   ) as T;
   50: }

 succeeded in 658ms:
    1: /**
    2:  * The config capability the suites hold — a reader over the at-config registry.
    3:  *
    4:  * This is NOT a stand-in for a later slice: the registry in `atconfig.ts` IS the single source
    5:  * for every pinned number, so the thing a test reaches through `h.config` is the real article and
    6:  * is registered as a real capability. There is no future version of it to wait for.
    7:  *
    8:  * Suites address values by DOTTED KEY (`req-015.thread_comment_notifications.max_per_window`)
    9:  * rather than by the registry's TypeScript identifier. The dotted key says which requirement owns
   10:  * the knob and what the knob is, which is what a test author is thinking about; the identifier is
   11:  * an implementation detail of the registry module. The map below is the only place the two meet.
   12:  *
   13:  * Two things throw rather than returning something usable:
   14:  *   - an unknown key, because a typo that silently returned undefined would make a cap of
   15:  *     `undefined` compare as "no cap" and the guard assertion would pass on nothing;
   16:  *   - a `null` value, i.e. a knob the requirement names but nobody has pinned. That doctrine is
   17:  *     the registry's own (`atconfig.ts`): an invented threshold is worse than a red.
   18:  */
   19: 
   20: import { AT_CONFIG, type AtConfigKey } from './atconfig.ts';
   21: import type { ConfigOverrides } from './registry.ts';
   22: 
   23: /** Every dotted key the suites may read, and the registry entry that pins it. */
   24: export const CONFIG_KEYS: Record<string, AtConfigKey> = {
   25:   'req-015.thread_comment_notifications.max_per_window': 'threadCommentNotificationsMaxPerWindow',
   26:   'req-015.thread_comment_notifications.window_ms': 'threadCommentNotificationsWindowMs',
   27:   'req-015.thread_comment_notifications.coalesce': 'threadCommentNotificationsCoalesce',
   28: };
   29: 
   30: export type ConfigRegistry = {
   31:   get<T>(key: string): T;
   32: };
   33: 
   34: /** Where an override names a knob that does not exist. Empty = every override is addressable. */
   35: export function unknownConfigKeys(overrides: ConfigOverrides): string[] {
   36:   return Object.keys(overrides)
   37:     .filter((key) => !(key in CONFIG_KEYS))
   38:     .sort();
   39: }
   40: 
   41: /**
   42:  * Build the reader, optionally with per-world overrides layered on top.
   43:  *
   44:  * Overrides may RE-TUNE a knob the registry already carries and may never introduce one. A test
   45:  * that could invent a key would be describing a configuration the product has no notion of, and
   46:  * "the implementation honours its configuration" would then be a claim about a knob nobody wired.
   47:  */
   48: export function createConfigRegistry(overrides: ConfigOverrides = {}): ConfigRegistry {
   49:   const unknown = unknownConfigKeys(overrides);
   50:   if (unknown.length) {
   51:     throw new Error(
   52:       `config override${unknown.length === 1 ? '' : 's'} ${unknown.join(', ')} name no at-config entry — ` +
   53:         `an override re-tunes a pinned value, it cannot invent one. Known keys: ${Object.keys(CONFIG_KEYS).sort().join(', ')}`,
   54:     );
   55:   }
   56: 
   57:   return {
   58:     get<T>(key: string): T {
   59:       const entryKey = CONFIG_KEYS[key];
   60:       if (!entryKey) {
   61:         throw new Error(
   62:           `no at-config entry is registered for ${JSON.stringify(key)}. Known keys: ${Object.keys(CONFIG_KEYS).sort().join(', ')}`,
   63:         );
   64:       }
   65:       if (key in overrides) return overrides[key] as T;
   66: 
   67:       const entry = AT_CONFIG[entryKey];
   68:       if (entry.value === null) {
   69:         throw new Error(
   70:           `${key} (${entry.name}) is not pinned anywhere — ${entry.source}. A test must fail on an ` +
   71:             `unpinned value rather than substitute a guess.`,
   72:         );
   73:       }
   74:       return entry.value as T;
   75:     },
   76:   };
   77: }

 succeeded in 693ms:
    1: /**
    2:  * The at-config registry — the single source for every pinned number the acceptance tests read.
    3:  *
    4:  * WHY: a threshold copied into a test body is a second source of truth. When the founder
    5:  * re-tunes a value, a hard-coded copy keeps asserting the old one and the suite goes green on
    6:  * a stale promise. Every configured number lives here; no test may hard-code one.
    7:  *
    8:  * `value: null` means the number is NOT pinned anywhere yet — the requirement names the knob
    9:  * but no figure exists. A test that reads a null must fail loudly rather than substitute a
   10:  * guess: an invented threshold is worse than a red.
   11:  *
   12:  * `provisional: true` marks a value that is not founder-settled — either flagged PROVISIONAL
   13:  * where it is stated, or an open decision (OD-4, OD-7). Provisional values are usable; they
   14:  * are expected to move.
   15:  *
   16:  * Sources are cited per entry. Adding a number here without a source is not allowed.
   17:  */
   18: 
   19: export interface AtConfigEntry {
   20:   /** plain-words name of the thing being pinned */
   21:   name: string;
   22:   /**
   23:    * The pinned figure, or null when no figure has been pinned anywhere. Booleans are pinned
   24:    * values too: whether a guard coalesces is configuration in exactly the way a cap is, and a
   25:    * test that hard-coded the switch would go stale the same way a hard-coded number does.
   26:    */
   27:   value: number | boolean | null;
   28:   unit: string;
   29:   /** not founder-settled — flagged PROVISIONAL at its source, or an open decision */
   30:   provisional?: true;
   31:   /** where the figure comes from, and what would settle it */
   32:   source: string;
   33: }
   34:

    1: /**
    2:  * The SHARED capability contract — one definition of every seam a suite is allowed to reach.
    3:  *
    4:  * Rule 5 of the suite-authoring rules (`loop/bringup/AI4DEV-3-at-harness.md`): tier, clock,
    5:  * fixture, sentinel, fault, config and vendor contracts come from ONE harness package, and a
    6:  * suite defines locally only its own system-under-test and fixture adapter. REQ-016's
    7:  * `_contract.ts` was the pre-harness stopgap — it declared the whole surface because no harness
    8:  * package existed to declare it — and thirty suites each restating the seam would drift into
    9:  * thirty subtly different seams, at which point "the harness contract" means nothing.
   10:  *
   11:  * These are types only. The implementations arrive per slice (H2 clock/fixtures, H3
   12:  * sentinels/faults, H4 oracles), and `pendingCapability()` in `index.ts` is what stands where one
   13:  * has not landed — a seam that throws the capability's name, never a no-op. Of the vendor sims, the
   14:  * EMAIL provider landed with AI4DEV-21 (`harness/vendors.ts`); the Anthropic usage/cost, Stripe,
   15:  * GitHub, Lovable and Linear stand-ins are each built with the FIRST test suite that consumes them
   16:  * (founder ruling, 2026-08-04), tracked as board items AI4DEV-38 through AI4DEV-42 under the
   17:  * AT-harness parent — their contracts land in this file when they do.
   18:  *

   37: /** A suite parameterizes this with its own world type; the seam is the same for all of them. */

   42: /**
   43:  * Time, under the test's control — funding's 7-day expiry, the UTC daily credit reset, blocker
   44:  * aging, abandonment, link expiry.
   45:  *
   46:  * There is deliberately NO `observedByProduct()` here. A method by which the clock reports its
   47:  * own time cannot distinguish a clock the product reads from one that only moves the test's
   48:  * notion of time — it is the clock answering a question about itself. That wiring is proven
   49:  * BEHAVIOURALLY instead: move the clock, then assert product behaviour that depends on time
   50:  * changed (the anti-spam window in AT-016.08). Sampling the clock from inside the product process
   51:  * is a real capability, but it needs a product process to sample, so it belongs to a later
   52:  * integration-tier slice, not to a self-report.
   53:  */
   54: export type Clock = {
   55:   freezeAt(iso: string): Promise<void>;
   56:   advance(ms: number): Promise<void>;
   57: };

   68:   /** scan a named store/scope for planted sentinels (presence AND absence) */
   69:   scan(scope: string): Promise<Sentinel[]>;

   72: export type FaultHandle = {
   73:   /** the point this handle is armed at — echoed back so a silent re-point is visible */
   74:   point: string;
   75:   /** how many times execution actually REACHED the armed point (0 = the fault never fired) */
   76:   triggerCount(): Promise<number>;
   77:   clear(): Promise<void>;
   78: };

   81:   /** the fault points the product actually exposes — arming an unknown point must not be a no-op */

   85:    * 'notifications.between_transition_and_event_write' is AT-016.09's point.
   86:    * MUST reject a point that is not in points().

  111:  * the vendor types are generic over the channel name rather than hard-coding one suite's set.
  112:  */
  113: export type ProviderAttempt<Channel extends string = string> = {
  114:   recipientId: string;
  115:   eventId: string;
  116:   channel: Channel;
  117:   outcome: ProviderOutcome;
  118: };

  120: export type EmailProviderSim<Channel extends string = string> = {
  121:   /** next N sends are rejected / never accepted (AT-016.11) */
  122:   rejectNext(count: number): void;
  123:   /** next N sends are ACCEPTED by the provider but the ack is lost (AT-016.11) */
  124:   acceptButLoseAck(count: number): void;
  125:   /** everything the provider actually accepted, in order */
  126:   accepted(): ProviderAttempt<Channel>[];
  127:   /**
  128:    * EVERY send that arrived at the provider seam, accepted or not, in order.
  129:    * This is the out-of-band trace: it is recorded by the simulator, not by the SUT, so it
  130:    * can contradict the SUT's own attempt counter (AT-016.11's retry proof, AT-016.01's
  131:    * "no path around the emitter").
  132:    */
  133:   attempts(): ProviderAttempt<Channel>[];
  134: };
  135: 
  136: export type Vendors<Channel extends string = string> = {
  137:   email: EmailProviderSim<Channel>;
  138: };
  139: 
  140: /* ----------------------------------------------------------------------- the harness */
  141: 
  142: /**
  143:  * What `createHarness()` hands a suite. A suite names its own system-under-test map and world
  144:  * type; everything else is the same for all thirty of them.
  145:  *
  146:  * A TYPE ALIAS, NOT AN INTERFACE, and that is load-bearing. An interface is open to declaration
  147:  * merging, so a suite could write `declare module './contracts.ts' { interface AtHarness { auditLog?: string[] } }`
  148:  * and then read `h.auditLog` with a green type-check, against a harness that supplies no such thing.
  149:  * The member being OPTIONAL is what makes it slip through: `createHarness()` still satisfies its
  150:  * annotation, so nothing anywhere goes red. That is the same lie a free harness type parameter used
  151:  * to permit, arriving by a different door and needing no `any` and no suppression. A type alias
  152:  * cannot be merged into, so this door is shut, and `tests/at/typeprobes/` keeps the attack on file
  153:  * as an executable negative test.
  154:  *
  155:  * EVERY CAPABILITY CONTRACT IN THIS FILE IS AN ALIAS FOR THE SAME REASON. Closing only the type
  156:  * below left the identical attack open one level down, and worse there: `sentinels`, `faults`,
  157:  * `static` and `vendors` come from `pendingCapability<T>()`, which casts a Proxy `as T`, so a
  158:  * merged-in member did not break `index.ts` even when it was REQUIRED — where the same member added
  159:  * to this type fails with TS2741. `ConfigRegistry` in `config.ts` is an alias for the same reason.
  160:  *
  161:  * So the rule is: contracts are type aliases, never interfaces. It covers everything reachable from
  162:  * the harness object AND the objects `open()` hands a test body — `OpenWorld`, `AtContext` and
  163:  * `WorldLike` in `registry.ts` obey it for the same reason these do. `WorldLike` was briefly
  164:  * excluded as belonging to AI4DEV-31's seam; that conflated two different defects. The suite's `W`
  165:  * being an unverified claim was AI4DEV-31's, and that item derived the seam types from the fixture
  166:  * adapter that produces them, leaving a suite no type argument to name either one with — though a
  167:  * widened context can still be REBUILT by hand out of the derived types, which is measured and
  168:  * documented on `SeamOpenWorld` in `registry.ts`. The interface being augmentable is this defect,
  169:  * not that one — and `w` is handed to the body exactly as `h` is.
  170:  *
  171:  * `tests/at/typeprobes/` carries an attack for every protected type, and
  172:  * `type-invention.selftest.ts` fails by name if any of them becomes an interface again.
  173:  */
  174: export type AtHarness<Sut = Record<string, unknown>, W extends WorldSeam = WorldSeam, Channel extends string = string> = {
  175:   tier: Tier;
  176:   /**
  177:    * Capability names this harness STUBBED for the running tier (e.g. 'vendors.email',
  178:    * 'sut.notifications'). MUST be empty above `loop` — otherwise an integration-tier run,
  179:    * which is the /pm-done gate, can silently stub the thing it is gating.
  180:    */
  181:   stubbedCapabilities(): Promise<string[]>;
  182:   clock: Clock;
  183:   fixtures: Fixtures<W>;
  184:   sentinels: Sentinels;
  185:   faults: Faults;
  186:   static: StaticScan;
  187:   config: ConfigRegistry;
  188:   vendors: Vendors<Channel>;
  189:   sut: Sut;
  190:   /** REQUIRED, not optional: frozen clocks, vendor counters and fault state leak without it */
  191:   teardown(): Promise<void>;
  192: };

   15: interface FixtureAdapter {
   16:   fixtures: { world(name: string): Promise<{ teardown(): Promise<void> }> };
   17:   sut: Record<string, unknown>;
   18:   /**
   19:    * OPTIONAL, and refused at use rather than ignored. The runner's own black-box trees write
   20:    * disposable adapters that export `sut`, `fixtures` and `teardown` and nothing else, so a
   21:    * required member here would break them at run time. Absence is not permission to no-op: an
   22:    * adapter that offers no fault seam exposes no fault points, so `faults.at()` refuses through
   23:    * `faultPointProblem` in the guard's own words, and a scan of any scope is refused the same way.
   24:    */
   25:   faults?: AdapterFaultSeam;
   26:   sentinels?: AdapterSentinelSeam;
   27:   teardown(): Promise<void>;
   28: }
   29: 
   30: interface FixtureAdapterModule {
   31:   /** the requirement this adapter declares itself to be, e.g. 'req-016' — see loadAdapter() */
   32:   requirement: string;
   33:   createFixtureAdapter(opts: {
   34:     clock: ControlledClock;
   35:     worlds: FixtureWorldStore;
   36:     config: ConfigRegistry;
   37:     /**
   38:      * The SUT-facing half of H5's provider seam. ADDITIVE on the options object rather than a new
   39:      * required export, so the runner's disposable black-box adapters — which take an options object
   40:      * they largely ignore — keep working untouched.
   41:      */
   42:     vendors: { email: EmailProviderPort };
   43:   }): Promise<FixtureAdapter> | FixtureAdapter;
   44: }
   45: 
   46: /**
   47:  * The adapter is resolved through REPO_ROOT rather than relative to this file, so that the suites
   48:  * directory the harness loads from is the SAME tree the runner and the bijection checker read.
   49:  * They agree by construction, including when `AT_REPO_ROOT` points all three at a disposable
   50:  * fixture tree for the runner's own black-box tests.
   51:  */
   52: function adapterUrl(requirement: string): string {
   53:   return pathToFileURL(join(REPO_ROOT, 'tests', 'at', 'suites', requirement, '_fixture.ts')).href;
   54: }
   55: 
   56: async function loadAdapter(
   57:   requirement: string,
   58:   clock: ControlledClock,
   59:   worlds: FixtureWorldStore,
   60:   config: ConfigRegistry,
   61:   vendors: { email: EmailProviderPort },
   62: ): Promise<FixtureAdapter> {
   63:   const moduleUrl = adapterUrl(requirement);
   64:   let module: Partial<FixtureAdapterModule>;
   65:   try {
   66:     module = (await import(/* @vite-ignore */ moduleUrl)) as Partial<FixtureAdapterModule>;
   67:   } catch (err) {
   68:     const detail = err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err);
   69:     throw new Error(`no fixture adapter for ${requirement} at ${moduleUrl} — ${detail}`);
   70:   }
   71:   if (typeof module.createFixtureAdapter !== 'function') {
   72:     throw new Error(`fixture adapter for ${requirement} exports no createFixtureAdapter()`);
   73:   }
   74: 
   75:   /*
   76:    * THE ADAPTER SAYS WHO IT IS, AND IS HELD TO IT.
   77:    *
   78:    * `harness/suite-adapters.ts` reads a suite's system-under-test and world types from the module
   79:    * its map names, while THIS function loads a module from a path built out of a string. Those were
   80:    * two independent facts: a mistyped map entry, a renamed directory or an `AT_REPO_ROOT` pointing
   81:    * somewhere else would have made the type-check describe one suite while the run drove another,
   82:    * and every layer would have looked correct on its own.
   83:    *
   84:    * The map entry is constrained to match this literal at compile time; the same literal is checked
   85:    * here against the requirement actually requested. So the key, the module the types came from and
   86:    * the module really imported are one self-declared value, checked at both ends.
   87:    *
   88:    * A MISSING literal is an error too, not a skipped check — a guard that switches itself off when a
   89:    * field is absent is the hole again, arriving through the door marked convenience.
   90:    */
   91:   if (module.requirement !== requirement) {
   92:     throw new Error(
   93:       `fixture adapter at ${moduleUrl} declares requirement ` +
   94:         `${module.requirement === undefined ? '<nothing: it exports no `requirement`>' : JSON.stringify(module.requirement)} ` +
   95:         `but was loaded as ${JSON.stringify(requirement)} — the suite's types would be read off one ` +
   96:         `module while the run drove another. Add \`export const requirement = ${JSON.stringify(requirement)} as const;\` ` +
   97:         `to that file, or correct whichever of the two names is wrong.`,
   98:     );
   99:   }
  100: 
  101:   return module.createFixtureAdapter({ clock, worlds, config, vendors });
  102: }
  103: 
  104: /**
  105:  * The return type is annotated, not inferred, and that annotation is load-bearing: it is what makes
  106:  * the shared contract a checked promise rather than a hopeful one. Inferred, this factory produced
  107:  * `object` for every pending seam — `pendingCapability<T extends object>()` has no inference site,
  108:  * so `T` fell back to its constraint — and the result was not assignable to `AtHarness` at all.
  109:  * With the annotation, dropping or misnaming a contract member is a compile error here, where it is
  110:  * written, instead of a surprise in whichever suite reaches for it.
  111:  */
  112: export async function createHarness(opts: {
  113:   requirement: string;
  114:   tier: Tier;
  115:   configOverrides?: ConfigOverrides;
  116: }): Promise<AtHarness> {
  117:   const clock = standInCapability('clock.controlled', new ControlledClock());
  118:   const worlds = new FixtureWorldStore(createFixtureSeed());
  119:   // REAL, not a stand-in: `atconfig.ts` IS the registry of pinned values, so what a test reads
  120:   // here is the article itself. Marking it stand-in would tell an integration-tier run that the
  121:   // gate is grading a substitute, which would be a lie in the other direction.
  122:   const config = realCapability('config.registry', createConfigRegistry(opts.configOverrides));
  123:   // BUILT BEFORE THE ADAPTER, because the adapter is handed the sending half of it. One sim per
  124:   // harness, so the forced-outcome queue and the accepted-identity set cannot survive into the next
  125:   // test — `registry.ts` opens a fresh harness per `open()` and tears it down per id.
  126:   const provider = createEmailProviderSim();
  127:   const adapter = await loadAdapter(opts.requirement, clock.value, worlds, config.value, { email: provider.port });
  128:   const fixtures = standInCapability('fixtures.worlds', adapter.fixtures);
  129:   // REAL, not stand-ins, for the same reason `config.registry` is. `stubbedCapabilities()` reports
  130:   // what the HARNESS substituted, and nothing about H3 is substituted here — the marker store and
  131:   // the fault router below are the article. What is a substitute in a loop-tier run is the product,
  132:   // and `sut.notifications` already declares that; declaring H3 a stand-in as well would count the
  133:   // same substitution twice and blame the wrong layer for it. It would also cost something real:
  134:   // `registry.ts` refuses ANY stubbed capability above the loop tier, so the label would bar this
  135:   // identical machinery from ever running at integration tier — the tier that is the closing gate.
  136:   const sentinels = realCapability('sentinels.planted', createSentinels(adapter.sentinels));
  137:   const faults = realCapability('faults.injection', createFaults(adapter.faults));
  138:   // A STAND-IN BY NATURE, and saying so has teeth: `registry.ts` refuses any stubbed capability
  139:   // above the loop tier, so an integration-tier run cannot silently grade against the simulator.
  140:   // What stands here at that tier is a later slice's decision, not this one's.
  141:   const vendors = standInCapability('vendors.email', { email: provider.sim });
  142:   const sutCapabilities: Capability<unknown>[] = Object.entries(adapter.sut).map(([name, value]) =>
  143:     standInCapability(`sut.${name}`, value),
  144:   );
  145:   const constructed: Capability<unknown>[] = [clock, fixtures, config, sentinels, faults, vendors, ...sutCapabilities];
  146: 
  147:   let tornDown = false;
  148:   return {
  149:     tier: opts.tier,
  150:     stubbedCapabilities: async () => stubbedCapabilityNames(constructed),
  151:     clock: clock.value,
  152:     fixtures: fixtures.value,
  153:     sut: adapter.sut,
  154:     sentinels: sentinels.value,
  155:     faults: faults.value,
  156:     // 'H3 sentinels' went from this list in the change that made planting work, and
  157:     // 'H5 email provider simulator' goes in the change that builds the simulator above. The seam
  158:     // names the whole missing set so its first throw reports all of it at once; the moment one of
  159:     // them lands, keeping its name here is a declared fact that has drifted from a real one.
  160:     // `AT-016.01` stays red — `providerClientImporters()` is what throws — but the reason it is red
  161:     // changed, and `tests/at/expected/req-016.json` states the same one name for the same reason.
  162:     static: pendingCapability<StaticScan>('H3 static provider scan'),
  163:     vendors: vendors.value,
  164:     config: config.value,
  165:     teardown: async () => {
  166:       if (tornDown) return;
  167:       tornDown = true;
  168:       await adapter.teardown();
  169:       await worlds.teardown();
  170:     },
  171:   };
  172: }

    6:   readonly [CAPABILITY]: true;
    7:   readonly name: string;
    8:   readonly provenance: CapabilityProvenance;
    9:   readonly value: T;
   10: }
   11: 
   12: function capability<T>(name: string, provenance: CapabilityProvenance, value: T): Capability<T> {
   13:   if (!name.trim()) throw new Error('a capability factory requires a non-empty name');
   14:   return Object.freeze({ [CAPABILITY]: true as const, name, provenance, value });
   15: }
   16: 
   17: export function realCapability<T>(name: string, value: T): Capability<T> {
   18:   return capability(name, 'real', value);
   19: }
   20: 
   21: export function standInCapability<T>(name: string, value: T): Capability<T> {
   22:   return capability(name, 'stand-in', value);
   23: }
   24: 
   25: export function stubbedCapabilityNames(capabilities: readonly Capability<unknown>[]): string[] {
   26:   return capabilities
   27:     .filter((entry) => entry[CAPABILITY] === true && entry.provenance === 'stand-in')
   28:     .map((entry) => entry.name)
   29:     .sort();
   30: }
   31: 
   32: export class CapabilityPending extends Error {
   33:   constructor(readonly capabilities: readonly string[]) {
   34:     super(`CAPABILITY PENDING — ${capabilities.join(', ')}`);
   35:     this.name = 'CapabilityPending';
   36:   }
   37: }
   38: 
   39: /** A typed seam for later slices. Any attempted use fails with the capability names, never a no-op. */
   40: export function pendingCapability<T extends object>(...capabilities: string[]): T {
   41:   const names = [...new Set(capabilities)];
   42:   return new Proxy(
   43:     {},
   44:     {
   45:       get() {
   46:         throw new CapabilityPending(names);
   47:       },
   48:     },
   49:   ) as T;

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:510: * Tear down everything the test built — worlds first, then harnesses,

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:511: * each — ATTEMPTING EVERY ONE and collecting the rejections rather than stopping

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:512: *
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:513: * Attempting all of them is the point: giving up after one rejection leaves the

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:514: * standing, and a frozen clock, an armed fault or a vendor counter that survives

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:516: */

> tests\at\harness\registry.ts:519:  const drain = async (stack: TrackedTeardown[]) => {
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:520:    while (stack.length) {
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:521:      const entry = stack.pop()!;
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:522:      try {
> tests\at\harness\registry.ts:523:        await entry.teardown();
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:524:      } catch (err) {
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:525:        failures.push({ what: entry.what, error: err });
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:526:      }
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:527:    }
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:528:  };
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:529:  await drain(worlds);
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:530:  await drain(harnesses);
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:531:  return failures;
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:532:}
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:533:
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:534:/**
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:535: * One acceptance test's whole lifecycle: run the body, tear everything down, then

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:536: *
> tests\at\harness\registry.ts:537: * WHY THE VERDICT LIVES HERE rather than in a `finally` that swallows teardown 
rejections —
> tests\at\harness\registry.ts:538: * which is what this used to do: a world whose teardown rejected did NOT release

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:539: * so the next id inherits it. Reporting that as green is a false green of the

  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:540: * the damage lands on a different test and the report blames the wrong id.
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:541: *
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:542: * When the body already failed, the body's error still wins the report: it is the 
more specific
> tests\at\harness\registry.ts:543: * fact, and a teardown that rejects because the body left the world half-built is 
a symptom, not
> tests\at\harness\registry.ts:544: * the cause. The teardown failures are printed so they are never invisible.
  tests\at\harness\registry.ts:545: */

  145: describe('taking over a stale lock is atomic — one owner, never two', () => {
  146:   /** A key of its own, so nothing here can disturb a real stack's lock. */
  147:   const testConfig = (): LocalConfig => ({ projectId: `selftest-${Math.random().toString(36).slice(2, 10)}`, apiPort: 1, dbPort: 2 });
  148: 
  149:   const plantLock = (config: LocalConfig, holder: Record<string, unknown>) => {
  150:     writeFileSync(stackLockPath(config), JSON.stringify(holder));
  151:   };
  152:   const scrub = (config: LocalConfig) => rmSync(stackLockPath(config), { force: true });
  153: 
  154:   it('takes over a lock whose holder is gone', () => {
  155:     const config = testConfig();
  156:     plantLock(config, { pid: 999_999, host: 'gone', requirement: 'req-000', startedAt: new Date().toISOString() });
  157:     try {
  158:       const lock = acquireStackLock(config, 'req-016');
  159:       expect(JSON.parse(readFileSync(lock.file, 'utf8')).pid, 'the takeover did not record this process as the holder').toBe(process.pid);
  160:       lock.release();
  161:       expect(existsSync(lock.file), 'release left the lock behind').toBe(false);
  162:     } finally {
  163:       scrub(config);
  164:     }
  165:   });
  166: 
  167:   it('refuses a live, fresh holder', () => {
  168:     const config = testConfig();
  169:     plantLock(config, { pid: process.pid, host: 'here', requirement: 'req-000', startedAt: new Date().toISOString() });
  170:     try {
  171:       expect(() => acquireStackLock(config, 'req-016')).toThrow(/another at:verify run holds this stack/);
  172:     } finally {
  173:       scrub(config);
  174:     }
  175:   });
  176: 
  177:   it('two contenders racing for ONE stale lock end with exactly one owner', async () => {
  178:     const config = testConfig();
  179:     // Stale by age as well as by liveness, so neither contender can decide otherwise.
  180:     plantLock(config, {
  181:       pid: 999_999,
  182:       host: 'gone',
  183:       requirement: 'req-000',
  184:       startedAt: new Date(Date.now() - 48 * 60 * 60 * 1000).toISOString(),
  185:     });
  186: 
  187:     const runnerUrl = new URL('./runner.ts', import.meta.url).href;
  188:     const startAt = Date.now() + 600;
  189:     const contender = (): Promise<string> => {
  190:       const code =
  191:         `const { acquireStackLock } = await import(${JSON.stringify(runnerUrl)});\n` +
  192:         `const config = ${JSON.stringify(JSON.stringify(config))};\n` +
  193:         `while (Date.now() < ${startAt}) {}\n` + // a barrier, so both attempt at the same instant
  194:         `try {\n` +
  195:         `  const lock = acquireStackLock(JSON.parse(config), 'req-016');\n` +
  196:         `  console.log('ACQUIRED');\n` +
  197:         `  await Bun.sleep(500);\n` + // hold it, so the loser meets a LIVE holder
  198:         `  lock.release();\n` +
  199:         `} catch { console.log('REFUSED'); }\n`;
  200:       // spawn, NOT spawnSync: a synchronous spawn would run the two contenders one after the
  201:       // other, and the second would find the lock already released — a race that never raced.
  202:       return new Promise<string>((resolve) => {
  203:         const child = spawn(bunExecutable(), ['--no-env-file', '-e', code], { stdio: ['ignore', 'pipe', 'pipe'] });
  204:         let out = '';
  205:         child.stdout.on('data', (chunk: Buffer) => (out += chunk.toString('utf8')));
  206:         child.stderr.on('data', (chunk: Buffer) => (out += chunk.toString('utf8')));
  207:         child.once('error', (err) => resolve(`ERROR ${err.message}`));
  208:         child.once('close', () => resolve(out));
  209:       });
  210:     };
  211: 
  212:     // Started together; the in-child barrier is what makes them collide, not the spawn timing.
  213:     const [a, b] = await Promise.all([contender(), contender()]);
  214:     const outcomes = [a, b].map((out) => (out.includes('ACQUIRED') ? 'ACQUIRED' : out.includes('REFUSED') ? 'REFUSED' : `UNKNOWN(${out.trim()})`));
  215: 
  216:     try {
  217:       expect(outcomes.filter((o) => o === 'ACQUIRED'), `outcomes were ${JSON.stringify(outcomes)}`).toHaveLength(1);
  218:       expect(outcomes.filter((o) => o === 'REFUSED'), `outcomes were ${JSON.stringify(outcomes)}`).toHaveLength(1);
  219:       expect(existsSync(stackLockPath(config)), 'the winner did not release its lock').toBe(false);
  220:     } finally {
  221:       scrub(config);
  222:     }
  223:   }, 60_000);
  224: });

  930: 
  931: /**
  932:  * End-of-run housekeeping. The lock release lives in a `finally` of its OWN so that it cannot be
  933:  * skipped: on Windows, removing the report directory can throw EPERM while a file in it is still
  934:  * open, and if that throw escaped, the stack lock would be stranded and every later run would
  935:  * find a leftover it has to reason about. A lost temp directory is untidy; a stranded lock blocks
  936:  * work, so the release always wins and the cleanup failure is reported instead of hidden.
  937:  */
  938: export function cleanupRun(reportDir: string, lock: { release(): void } | null): void {
  939:   try {
  940:     rmSync(reportDir, { recursive: true, force: true });
  941:   } catch (err) {
  942:     console.error(`at:verify — could not remove the report directory ${reportDir}: ${diagnostic((err as Error).message)}`);
  943:   } finally {
  944:     lock?.release();
  945:   }
  946: }
  947: 
  948: function firstLine(text: string | undefined, fallback: string): string {
  949:   const line = redact(text ?? '')
  950:     .split('\n')
  951:     .map((l) => l.trim())
  952:     .find((l) => l.length > 0);
  953:   return line ?? fallback;
  954: }

    1: # Environment variables — what goes where.
    2: #
    3: # The tracked `.env` holds PUBLIC values only: the Supabase project id, the project URL, and
    4: # the publishable key. All three are designed to be public — they ship in the browser bundle
    5: # and are protected by row-level security, not by secrecy. That file stays in git.
    6: #
    7: # Any SECRET — the Supabase service-role key, provider API keys (Anthropic, Stripe, GitHub,
    8: # Linear, Lovable), webhook signing secrets — goes in `.env.local`, which is git-ignored and
    9: # is never committed. If a value would do damage in a stranger's hands, it belongs there.
   10: #
   11: # WHY `.env` STAYS TRACKED, against Supabase's own advice (founder decision, 2026-07-29).
   12: # Supabase documents that `config.toml` can pull secrets via `env(NAME)` from the root `.env`,
   13: # and therefore says do not commit it. We keep it tracked anyway, for one reason: Lovable owns
   14: # the frontend and its build needs those VITE_ values present in the repo. The tradeoff is
   15: # accepted knowingly, with two mitigations:
   16: #   1. The test harness no longer inherits the parent environment — it passes an allowlisted
   17: #      env to its children, so a secret sitting in `.env`/`.env.local` cannot reach a test and
   18: #      from there reach the hosted project. (See tests/at/harness/runner.ts.)
   19: #   2. The rule above is the discipline that keeps it safe: NOTHING secret goes in `.env`.
   20: #      If a Supabase feature ever needs `env()` interpolation for a real secret, that is the
   21: #      moment to revisit this decision — not before.
   22: #
   23: # This file lists the variable names only, with empty values, as the shape to copy.

    1: /**
    2:  * The type-invention attacks, kept dead by an executable test rather than by memory.
    3:  *
    4:  * AI4DEV-24 exists to make `bun run typecheck` mean something. The thing that would quietly undo it
    5:  * is a suite declaring members no runtime value supplies and reading them green — a type-check that
    6:  * lies, which is worse than no type-check at all because it is believed.
    7:  *
    8:  * Two families live here now. AI4DEV-24's is HARNESS invention: a suite declaring seams
    9:  * `createHarness()` never produces. AI4DEV-31's is SUT-AND-WORLD invention: a suite declaring what
   10:  * its system under test and its fixture world are, when the adapter that really supplies them says
   11:  * otherwise. None of the attacks needs `any` and none needs a suppression, so grepping for those
   12:  * would not have caught any of them.
   13:  *
   14:  * THE PROBES ARE COMPILED PER FILE, not only as one program, and that is load-bearing. A combined
   15:  * exit code is satisfied by ANY error anywhere: a protection could be removed and the suite would
   16:  * stay green on the strength of its neighbours' diagnostics. Each probe file therefore has its own
   17:  * committed child config, and each protection is asserted against the output of the file that
   18:  * attacks it.
   19:  */
   20: 
   21: import { spawnSync } from 'node:child_process';
   22: import { isAbsolute } from 'node:path';
   23: import { describe, expect, it } from 'vitest';
   24: 
   25: import { INSTALL_ROOT } from './check.ts';
   26: import { pinnedTsc } from '../typecheck.ts';
   27: 
   28: const PROBE_PROJECT = 'tests/at/typeprobes/tsconfig.json';
   29: /** AI4DEV-31's attacks in the API that ALLOWED them — the red half of that item's proof. */
   30: const LEGACY_SEAM_PROJECT = 'tests/at/typeprobes/tsconfig.sut-seam-legacy.json';
   31: /** AI4DEV-31's attacks in the API that REPLACED it — only meaningful after the change. */
   32: const SEAM_PROJECT = 'tests/at/typeprobes/tsconfig.sut-seam.json';
   33: 
   34: interface ProbeRun {
   35:   status: number | null;
   36:   output: string;
   37:   /** every file tsc actually put in the program — the control against an empty-program exit 0 */
   38:   files: string[];
   39: }
   40: 
   41: function typecheckProbes(project: string): ProbeRun {
   42:   // The same pinned compiler `bun run typecheck` uses. A negative test is only meaningful if the
   43:   // compiler rejecting the attack is the compiler that will check everything else.
   44:   //
   45:   // `--listFiles` is not decoration: a tsconfig that matches NO files also exits 0 and reports
   46:   // nothing, so "the attack was rejected" and "the attack was never compiled" are indistinguishable
   47:   // without it. Every per-file assertion below leans on `compiled()` to rule that out.
   48:   const result = spawnSync(
   49:     process.execPath,
   50:     [pinnedTsc(INSTALL_ROOT), '--noEmit', '--pretty', 'false', '--listFiles', '-p', project],
   51:     { cwd: INSTALL_ROOT, encoding: 'utf8' },
   52:   );
   53:   if (result.error) throw new Error(`could not run the compiler over ${project}: ${result.error.message}`);
   54:   const raw = `${result.stdout ?? ''}${result.stderr ?? ''}`;
   55:   const lines = raw.split('\n');
   56:   // `--listFiles` interleaves the program's files with the diagnostics. The listed files are
   57:   // ABSOLUTE, while diagnostics name their file relative to the cwd and their continuation lines are
   58:   // indented — so absoluteness separates the two without guessing at message text.
   59:   //
   60:   // The absoluteness test has to be THE RUNNING PLATFORM'S. This read `/^[A-Za-z]:[\\/]/`, which is
   61:   // a Windows path and only a Windows path: on Linux every listed file begins with `/`, none matched,
   62:   // `files` came back empty and the two controls below failed on CI while passing locally — the
   63:   // controls doing their job, on the wrong question. `isAbsolute` is win32's on Windows and posix's
   64:   // on Linux, which is the same platform whose compiler printed the line.
   65:   const isListedFile = (line: string) => isAbsolute(line);
   66:   return {
   67:     status: result.status,
   68:     output: lines.filter((line) => !isListedFile(line)).join('\n'),
   69:     files: lines.filter(isListedFile).map((line) => line.trim()),
   70:   };
   71: }
   72: 
   73: /** Did tsc really read this probe? Guards every assertion below against a program matching nothing. */
   74: function compiled(run: ProbeRun, probeFile: string): boolean {
   75:   return run.files.some((file) => file.endsWith(probeFile));
   76: }
   77: 
   78: /**
   79:  * TypeScript reports a blocked merge two different ways: `TS2300 Duplicate identifier 'X'` for a
   80:  * single clash, and one `TS6200` listing every identifier when a file has several at once. Both are
   81:  * the rejection these tests assert, so either is accepted — matching only the first form would make
   82:  * a test pass or fail on how many attacks happen to share a file.
   83:  */
   84: function rejects(output: string, name: string): boolean {
   85:   if (output.includes(`Duplicate identifier '${name}'`)) return true;
   86:   return output
   87:     .split('\n')
   88:     .filter((line) => line.includes('conflict with those in another file:'))
   89:     .some((line) => line.split(':').pop()!.split(',').some((entry) => entry.trim() === name));
   90: }
   91: 
   92: describe('harness invention is rejected by the compiler', () => {
   93:   const probe = typecheckProbes(PROBE_PROJECT);
   94: 
   95:   it('compiles the probes it claims to check', () => {
   96:     // The empty-program control. A config that matches nothing exits 0 and says nothing, so without
   97:     // this every assertion below could be passing because the attack was never read.
   98:     expect(compiled(probe, 'harness-invention.probe.ts'), `${PROBE_PROJECT} did not compile the probe file`).toBe(true);
   99:   });
  100: 
  101:   it('the probe program does not compile — if it ever does, an attack has reopened', () => {
  102:     expect(
  103:       probe.status,
  104:       `${PROBE_PROJECT} compiled clean. Every construct in it is a way for a suite to declare harness ` +
  105:         `members the factory never produces; a green run means one of them is legal again and the ` +
  106:         `type-check is once more able to promise a runtime shape nothing establishes.\n${probe.output}`,
  107:     ).not.toBe(0);
  108:   });
  109: 
  110:   it('rejects a harness type invented through an intersection', () => {
  111:     // `AtHarness & { auditLog }` is a SUBTYPE of AtHarness, so it satisfies any `extends` constraint.
  112:     // The defence is that the harness is not a type parameter at all — hence an arity error.
  113:     expect(probe.output, 'the intersection attack was not rejected by arity').toContain('TS2558');
  114:   });
  115: 
  116:   /**
  117:    * EVERY type that must stay a type alias, and why the list is exhaustive rather than a sample.
  118:    *
  119:    * The rule is uniform — nothing reachable from the harness object, or from the wrapper handed to a
  120:    * test body, may be an interface — so the guard is uniform too. An earlier version of this test
  121:    * asserted three of these and claimed to assert all of them; both Gate 2 reviewers caught that the
  122:    * guard was narrower than its own comment, which is exactly the reversion such a gap invites.
  123:    *
  124:    * THIS ARRAY IS THE SPECIFICATION. The probe file has to carry an augmentation for every name in
  125:    * it, because a name listed here but not attacked there produces no diagnostic and fails its test.
  126:    * So the two files cannot drift apart silently.
  127:    */
  128:   const ALIAS_PROTECTED = [
  129:     // the harness contract itself
  130:     'AtHarness',
  131:     // the capability contracts it references (contracts.ts)
  132:     'WorldSeam',
  133:     'Fixtures',
  134:     'Clock',
  135:     'Sentinel',
  136:     'Sentinels',
  137:     'FaultHandle',
  138:     'Faults',
  139:     'StaticScan',
  140:     'ProviderAttempt',
  141:     'EmailProviderSim',
  142:     'Vendors',
  143:     // the config seam (config.ts)
  144:     'ConfigRegistry',
  145:     // the wrapper types every test body holds directly (registry.ts) — including the world itself,
  146:     // because `open()` hands `w` to the body exactly as it hands `h`
  147:     'OpenWorld',
  148:     'AtContext',
  149:     'WorldLike',
  150:   ];
  151: 
  152:   for (const contract of ALIAS_PROTECTED) {
  153:     it(`rejects a member merged into ${contract}`, () => {
  154:       expect(
  155:         rejects(probe.output, contract),
  156:         `${contract} accepted a merged-in member, so it is an interface again. A suite can now declare ` +
  157:           `a member on it and read that member green against a value that never supplies it.\n${probe.output}`,
  158:       ).toBe(true);
  159:     });
  160:   }
  161: });
  162: 
  163: /**
  164:  * AI4DEV-31's half: the system under test and the fixture world are the ADAPTER's to declare, and no
  165:  * longer the suite's to claim.
  166:  *
  167:  * Two probe files, compiled separately, because one file cannot carry both halves of the proof. The
  168:  * legacy probe is written in the API that ALLOWED the lie — at the pre-fix commit it compiled with
  169:  * exit 0, which is `loop/items/AI4DEV-31/proof-red.txt`; the same file must fail now. The new probe
  170:  * is written in the API that replaced it and is only meaningful afterwards.
  171:  *
  172:  * Every diagnostic asserted below was READ OFF THE COMPILER, never predicted. The design for this
  173:  * item predicted TS2558 by analogy with AI4DEV-24's arity defence and the real code is TS2344,
  174:  * because the new `bindSuite` still takes two type parameters — they are just no longer shapes. A

  192: /* --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- redaction */
  193: 
  194: /** Strip anything key-shaped out of text that is about to be printed. */
  195: export function redact(text: string): string {
  196:   return String(text ?? '')
  197:     .replace(/eyJ[A-Za-z0-9_-]{5,}\.[A-Za-z0-9_-]{5,}\.[A-Za-z0-9_-]{5,}/g, '<redacted-jwt>')
  198:     .replace(/\bsb_[a-z]+_[A-Za-z0-9_-]{8,}/g, '<redacted-key>')
  199:     .replace(/(postgres(?:ql)?:\/\/)[^@\s/]+@/gi, '$1<redacted>@')
  200:     .replace(/[A-Za-z0-9_-]{40,}/g, '<redacted-token>');
  201: }
  202: 
  203: /** First non-empty line, redacted and length-capped — enough to diagnose, not enough to leak. */
  204: function diagnostic(text: string | undefined, limit = 400): string {
  205:   const line =
  206:     redact(text ?? '')
  207:       .split('\n')
  208:       .map((l) => l.trim())
  209:       .find((l) => l.length > 0) ?? '';
  210:   return line.length > limit ? `${line.slice(0, limit)}…` : line;
  211: }
  212: 
  213: /* ------------------------------------------------------------------------ config.toml reading */
  214: 
  215: export interface LocalConfig {
  216:   projectId: string;
  217:   apiPort: number;
  218:   dbPort: number;
  219: }
  220: 
  221: /** Ports and project id come from `supabase/config.toml` — never guessed, never hard-coded. */
  222: function readLocalConfig(): LocalConfig {
  223:   const file = join(REPO_ROOT, 'supabase', 'config.toml');
  224:   const text = readFileSync(file, 'utf8');
  225:   let section = '';
  226:   let projectId = '';
  227:   let apiPort = 0;
  228:   let dbPort = 0;
  229: 
  230:   for (const raw of text.split('\n')) {
  231:     const line = raw.trim();
  232:     const header = /^\[([^\]]+)\]/.exec(line);
  233:     if (header) {
  234:       section = header[1];
  235:       continue;
  236:     }
  237:     const port = /^port\s*=\s*(\d+)/.exec(line);
  238:     if (section === '' && /^project_id\s*=/.test(line)) projectId = /"([^"]+)"/.exec(line)?.[1] ?? '';
  239:     else if (section === 'api' && port && !apiPort) apiPort = Number(port[1]);
  240:     else if (section === 'db' && port && !dbPort) dbPort = Number(port[1]);
  241:   }
  242: 
  243:   const missing = [projectId ? '' : 'project_id', apiPort ? '' : '[api] port', dbPort ? '' : '[db] port'].filter(Boolean);
  244:   if (missing.length) throw new Error(`${file} is missing ${missing.join(' and ')}`);
  245:   return { projectId, apiPort, dbPort };
  246: }

# Gate 1 critique — AI4DEV-20

## Verdict

BLOCKING FINDINGS PRESENT — the hard integration gate remains nondeterministic, and the plan cannot prove that committed replay data came from the declared live oracle.

## Findings

1. [severity BLOCKER] — §3c determinism — Three live votes reduce random variance but cannot guarantee that identical input receives the same verdict on different days; correlated model or infrastructure changes defeat the stated `≈3p²` argument. Replay is restricted to loop while integration—the closing gate—uses live calls. Evidence: `plan.md:21-25,101-112`; `loop/bringup/AI4DEV-3-at-harness.md:32-35`; `registry.ts:617-619`. Fix: either use a genuinely deterministic pinned oracle at integration, explicitly change the tier/spec contract to permit reviewed replay, or narrow the guarantee with founder approval and add a measured stability bound. Majority alone is not determinism.

2. [severity BLOCKER] — §§4f, 4h, 5.6–5.7 — The plan requires committed recordings but defines no recording command or sole writer, while the only live generation step is optional. Without a key, the executor can satisfy the stated greens only by committing synthetic/canned “recordings,” which would not prove the declared live judge. Evidence: `plan.md:150-151,182-204,215-216`. Fix: define one explicit recorder that canonicalizes inputs, calls the live transport, validates the response, writes atomically, and records requested/served model provenance; require successful live recording before merge or remove live-backed grounding recordings from this item.

3. [severity MAJOR] — §4c tier wiring — The assertion that a real oracle makes `stubbedCapabilities()` empty above loop is false in this tree. `createHarness()` unconditionally registers the clock, fixtures, email vendor, and every SUT member as stand-ins. Integration is rejected before any suite can exercise the oracle. Evidence: `index.ts:117,128,141-145`; `registry.ts:617-619`; contrary claim at `plan.md:110-112`. The exact loop ledger at `conformance.selftest.ts:154-159` must also gain `oracles.judge`, which the plan does not name. Fix: state that only oracle provenance is being established, add direct loop/live oracle factory tests, update the exact loop ledger, and explicitly test that live mode never reads replay. Do not claim full integration reachability until the other stand-ins are replaced.

4. [severity MAJOR] — §§3c.2, 4a–4b — The extraction contract is not executable as specified. It says code performs minimum/tolerance comparisons, but the proposed criterion shape contains no expected value, comparator, tolerance, extraction result type, unit, or normalization rule; `judge()` receives only rubric and material. Evidence: `plan.md:96-100,124-129`; AT-033 requires minimum-event, no-fabrication, and runway-tolerance behavior at `.taskmaster/docs/acceptance/at-req-033.md:21`. Fix: define a serializable discriminated extraction criterion with typed expected values, comparison operations, tolerances, units, and normalization, then pin boundary cases in conformance tests.

5. [severity MAJOR] — §§2, 4f — The grounding-rubric mitigation is decorative for AT-004.10 and AT-033.07. Their fixture facts, event set, minimum, and tolerance do not exist yet, so a rubric authored now cannot be “derived line-by-line” into the final fixture-specific oracle. This conflicts with the parent’s just-in-time boundary and its warning against contracts built before consumers. Evidence: `plan.md:29-44,182-185`; `loop/bringup/AI4DEV-3-at-harness.md:58-64,168-172`; `.taskmaster/docs/acceptance/at-req-004.md:25`; `.taskmaster/docs/acceptance/at-req-033.md:21`. Fix: use requirement-neutral conformance rubrics now and leave requirement rubrics to their suites, or explicitly label these as disposable examples rather than proof of the final shape.

6. [severity MAJOR] — §§3c.4, 4b — The replay key omits behavior-changing request inputs: effort, thinking configuration, output schema, maximum tokens, canonicalization version, and the actual rendered prompt. A manually maintained `PROMPT_VERSION` does not make prompt edits invalidate recordings “by construction”; forgetting to bump it silently reuses stale data. Evidence: `plan.md:76-79,107-108,141-143`. Fix: hash a canonical serialization of the complete rendered request and schema, including material slot names and values, rather than relying on a hand-bumped prompt constant.

7. [severity MAJOR] — §3b API decision — `effort: "low"` is supported, but the settled claim that it is documented as strong for this task and reduces verdict variance is unsupported. Anthropic recommends starting at `high` and lowering effort where task-specific evaluations show quality holds. The plan has no consuming suite or mandatory live evaluation establishing that. Its statement that sending any sampling parameter causes a 400 is also broader than the migration documentation, which specifies non-default values. Evidence: `plan.md:49-56,76-79`; [Anthropic effort guidance](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/effort), [Anthropic migration guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/migration-guide). Fix: keep effort provisional, run a labeled effort sweep, and include the selected effort in the replay key.

8. [severity MAJOR] — §4d credential path — Passing `AT_JUDGE_API_KEY` to the Vitest child materially weakens the current leak model: the tree promises that provider secrets cannot reach tests, while the spawned test process inherits stdout/stderr directly and could print or misuse the key. Every test in every non-loop requirement receives it, not only semantic-oracle calls. Evidence: `.env.example:7-18`; `runner.ts:113-121,163-169,1121-1139`; `plan.md:165-172`. Fix: keep the key outside Vitest—prefer a bounded parent-side judge/recorder broker—or require an explicit, narrowly scoped live-oracle mode with a limited key and update the threat model.

9. [severity MAJOR] — §§4d, 4g — The proposed credential sentinel can green without testing the real spawn condition. `childEnv(extra)` accepts and spreads any caller-supplied value, so testing `childEnv({AT_JUDGE_API_KEY: ...})` proves only that generic mechanism, not that the Vitest call passes it exclusively for non-loop tiers. Evidence: `runner.ts:163-169,1137`; current real-child probes at `runner.selftest.ts:44-84`. Fix: extract the exact tier-dependent environment builder used by the spawn site and run real-child probes for loop, integration, and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` exclusion.

10. [severity MAJOR] — §§3c.3, 4e, 4g — The conformance plan does not prove the registry is the sole writer of vote count. A literal `3` could remain in `oracles.ts` while the registry entry and its self-test both pass. Overrides can also supply zero, negative, fractional, or even vote counts, leaving ties or no verdict undefined. Evidence: `plan.md:101-103,174-178,190-195`; `registry.ts:147-148`; `config.ts:42-48`. Fix: construct the oracle from `h.config`, validate a positive odd integer, and prove through overrides that transport call count and aggregation actually change. Add crossed multi-criterion votes and an explicit live-never-replay test.

11. [severity MAJOR] — §§4a, 5.1 — Merely keeping existing type-invention probes green does not protect the new exported oracle types. The current executable specification enumerates every protected alias; none of the proposed `Rubric`, criterion, verdict, provenance, or oracle types will be attacked unless both the probe and list are extended. Evidence: `contracts.ts:146-172`; `type-invention.selftest.ts:116-150`; insufficient criterion at `plan.md:208`. Fix: add every new reachable contract alias to `ALIAS_PROTECTED` and add matching declaration-merging attacks.

12. [severity MINOR] — §§4b, 4g — “Teardown leaves no state” has no implementation path or observable criterion. Current harness teardown releases only the adapter and fixture store; no oracle resource is registered. Evidence: `index.ts:165-169`; teardown accounting at `registry.ts:510-575`; promise at `plan.md:195`. Fix: declare the oracle stateless and prove fresh-instance isolation, or return a disposer from its factory, invoke it from harness teardown, and test that disposer failures make the run red.

No additional finding: the current model ID, `output_config.format` structured-output shape, refusal/max-token handling, lack of a seed option, and test-only SDK dependency placement are workable; the fixed dateless model ID is a pinned snapshot, although serving infrastructure may still vary. [Anthropic structured outputs](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/structured-outputs), [model-ID guarantees](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/model-ids-and-versions). Keeping `tests/at/expected/req-016.json` byte-identical is also achievable.

## Residual risks accepted if the plan proceeds unchanged

- Same-provider self-preference and systematic rubric bias remain correlated across all three votes.
- Provider availability, rate limits, model access, and cost can make the hard gate unavailable.
- The SDK officially documents Node.js 18+ rather than Bun as its supported runtime; an optional smoke leaves that compatibility unproved.
- One compliant and one violating specimen per rubric cannot expose criteria the judge silently ignores.
- Future rubric or request changes can leave committed recordings stale or require widespread re-recording.
